## Resolved on your direction (2026-09-14, "pick up remaining item in stage 13")

### Stage 13's remaining item — "industry compliance packs as a real, separately-metered paid feature" and "further account-management build-out" — closed out; two genuine correctness bugs found and fixed along the way
**Parked:** 2026-09-10, in the Stage 13 usage-limit entry above, as not requested that round. **Resolved:** 2026-09-14, per your direction to pick it up, with two scoping questions answered first: the "compliance pack" paid feature is the Quarterly Audit (CO Quarterly Report) and Annual Independent Review, not multi-industry packs; account-management build-out is both self-service plan changes and org member role management.
**What changed:**
- **Compliance-pack reinterpretation, reusing existing infrastructure rather than building new:** `app/models/billing.py` already had a full `AddonKey`/`ADDON_CATALOGUE`/`SubscriptionAddon`/`has_addon()`/`purchase_addon()` mechanism (built for `enterprise_crypto_screening`), including an `independent_review` add-on entry that was defined but never actually gated anywhere. Added `AddonKey.quarterly_compliance_report` alongside it (same "TBA" pricing pattern — priced separately, not yet set, disclosed rather than invented) and wired `has_addon()` checks into the two creation points: `independent_review.py::create_review()` (requires the `independent_review` add-on) and `board_reporting.py::create_report()` when `report_type == quarterly_compliance` (requires the new add-on) — `board_aml`/`risk_committee`/`annual_aml` stay free, unchanged. Purchase/cancel already worked generically via the existing `/billing/addons/{addon_key}/purchase|cancel` endpoints, so no new billing UI plumbing was needed for the gate itself.
- **Real billing correctness bug found investigating "self-service plan upgrade/downgrade" before assuming it needed building:** the backend (`/billing/checkout`, `/billing/portal`, real Stripe Checkout Session + Customer Portal integration) and frontend (`web/app/billing/page.tsx`) were already fully built and wired — but `create_checkout_session()` always creates a brand-new Stripe subscription (`mode="subscription"` Checkout Sessions have no "replace the existing one" concept). An already-subscribed org calling `/checkout` for a different plan — which the UI allowed, since every plan card's button called the same `checkout()` handler regardless of current subscription state — would silently double-bill: the old Stripe subscription is never cancelled. Fixed by rejecting `create_checkout_session()` for an org with an active/trialing/past-due subscription, and adding `change_subscription_plan()` (modifies the existing Stripe subscription's price in place via `stripe.Subscription.modify()`, with proration) plus `POST /billing/subscription/change-plan`. The webhook handler (`_handle_subscription_updated`) now also resyncs `plan`/`interval` from the subscription's current Stripe price via a new `_plan_interval_for_price()` reverse lookup, so a plan change made directly in the Stripe Customer Portal (bypassing our own endpoint) still lands in the DB. `web/app/billing/page.tsx`'s `checkout()` now branches on the org's current subscription status — change-plan (no redirect) for an existing subscriber, checkout (Stripe redirect) for a new one.
- **Real access-control gap found investigating "org member role management" before assuming a whole new page was needed:** `add_member()`/`update_member()`/`remove_member()`/`list_members()` already existed with real RBAC (`org:manage` permission), and an "owner" system role was already seeded on org creation (`attach_owner()`) — but "owner" and "admin" are both seeded with every permission (`{"*"}`), and nothing stopped an org's last owner from being demoted, suspended, or removed via the ordinary member endpoints, leaving the org with no one who could grant "owner" back through the product itself (only a platform super-admin could rescue it, out-of-band). Added `count_active_owners()` (`app/services/org_service.py`) and a last-owner guard on `update_member()`/`remove_member()` (409, pointing at the new endpoint instead), plus a dedicated `POST /organisations/{org_id}/transfer-ownership` — deliberately restricted to the *current* owner alone (not just any `org:manage` holder, even though "admin" holds the same permissions), matching how real SaaS products treat ownership transfer as a deliberate handover rather than routine member management. No frontend team-management page exists at all yet (checked — there's no page calling `list_members`/`add_member` anywhere in `web/app`); left as a disclosed, larger, separate gap rather than building one unprompted this round.
**What did NOT change / known scope limits (disclosed, not fixed this round):** `independent_review`'s and `quarterly_compliance_report`'s add-on pricing remain "TBA" (a business decision, not invented); no frontend page surfaces the org member management API at all (add/update/remove/transfer), so today it's API-only; `purchase_addon()` still doesn't create a real Stripe charge for either add-on (consistent with how `independent_review` already worked before this round — a DB-only entitlement toggle).
**Verified:** 13 new tests (`tests/test_p13_addon_gating_smoke.py`, 6 tests: gated/ungated creation for both report types; `tests/test_p13_account_management_smoke.py`, 7 tests: checkout mock-mode behaviour, change-plan success/404, last-owner removal/demotion/suspension all rejected, successful transfer then the ex-owner can be removed, non-owner transfer rejected). 3 pre-existing tests that created an Independent Review or a Quarterly Compliance Report without an add-on needed updating to grant one first (`tests/test_independent_review_target_completion_smoke.py`, `tests/test_ir_and_co_quarterly_template_alignment_smoke.py`, `tests/test_p29_audit_coverage_sweep_smoke.py`) — a real, expected interaction with the new gate, not a bug. Full suite: 652 passed. `npx tsc --noEmit` clean on the whole `web/` app. Ruff (check + format) clean on all changed/added Python files; mypy's diff against pre-change output shows exactly 10 new errors, all the same already-tolerated `Column(...) → X | None` pattern used ~118 times already in these same two files (tracked separately as P5), confirmed by a message-normalised diff rather than assumed.
**Detail:** `app/models/billing.py`; `app/services/billing_service.py`; `app/services/org_service.py`; `app/schemas/billing.py`; `app/schemas/organisation.py`; `app/api/routes/billing.py`; `app/api/routes/independent_review.py`; `app/api/routes/board_reporting.py`; `app/api/routes/organisations.py`; `web/app/billing/page.tsx`; `tests/test_p13_addon_gating_smoke.py` (new); `tests/test_p13_account_management_smoke.py` (new); `tests/test_independent_review_target_completion_smoke.py`; `tests/test_ir_and_co_quarterly_template_alignment_smoke.py`; `tests/test_p29_audit_coverage_sweep_smoke.py`.

### AML Program export document-control policy (watermark / 1-year expiry / print-blocked) — implemented in real code, not just decided on paper
**Decided:** 2026-09-14, resolving item 02 of the Monetisation Playbook artifact's §06 open-decisions checklist ("add watermark until unable to OCR, document expiring date 1 year only if download, not allow to print, printed copy is uncontrolled"). **Resolved:** 2026-09-14, same day, per your direction ("yes actual implement") that the policy be real code, not just artifact text.
**What changed:** The existing `POST /organisations/{org_id}/aml-program/export` returns JSON only and has never produced an actual document — no PDF/HTML generation, watermark, print control, or expiry tracking existed anywhere in the codebase before this. Added `GET /organisations/{org_id}/aml-program/export-html` (`app/api/routes/organisations.py`), following the browser-print-to-PDF convention already used by `board_reporting.py`/`independent_review.py`'s `export-html` endpoints: (1) unpaid/preview orgs (`full_aml_program` not entitled) get a dense, small, tiled, rotated SVG-text watermark as a repeating CSS background (data: URI, not a DOM `url(#id)` reference — confirmed unreliable cross-browser for inline SVG patterns) — the tile is small/dense enough to overlap actual content glyph strokes rather than sit only in whitespace, so a screenshot-then-OCR pass sees interleaved watermark and content strokes rather than cleanly separable text; (2) every export, paid or unpaid, is stamped with a validity date computed as `datetime.now(timezone.utc) + timedelta(days=365)` at the moment of *this* download (not at program-generation time, per "only if download"); (3) `@media print` hides all real content (`.watermark`, `.cover`, `.content`) and leaves only a "Printing disabled" notice plus the uncontrolled-copy footer visible, so a printed page (or print-to-PDF) can never carry the actual program content; (4) that same uncontrolled-copy footer text is the one thing left standing if print-blocking is ever bypassed. Every download requires a non-empty `reason` (400 otherwise) and is audit-logged (`action="aml_program_export_html_downloaded"`, `after_state` includes `downloaded_at`/`expires_at`/`is_preview`). Wired the frontend: `web/app/aml-program/page.tsx`'s "Export with audit trail" flow previously called the old JSON-only endpoint and showed a static "Export complete" message that delivered no document at all — now calls the new endpoint (`exportAmlProgramHtml()`, `web/lib/signup.ts`) and opens the returned HTML as a Blob URL in a new tab (opened synchronously on click, before the `await`, so browsers don't treat it as an unsolicited popup), so the user actually receives the watermarked, expiry-stamped, print-controlled document.
**What did NOT change / known scope limits (disclosed, not fixed this round):** the export is HTML styled for browser print-to-PDF, not a server-generated PDF file (no PDF library exists in the codebase; this matches the existing `board_reporting`/`independent_review` convention exactly, so it's a consistency choice, not a shortcut unique to this feature); the watermark/print-block is CSS/browser-native, not a hardened DRM — a technically sophisticated user could still defeat it (e.g. via browser devtools), which is disclosed rather than claimed as unbreakable.
**Verified:** 5 new tests (`tests/test_p13_aml_program_document_control_smoke.py`) — unpaid-org watermark + DRAFT badge present, paid-org watermark + badge absent, missing reason rejected 400, one-year validity date stamped and print-block CSS present, download audited with the reason recorded. Also visually verified via Playwright (not just test assertions): screenshotted both the normal watermarked view (watermark strokes visibly overlapping table text) and the print-media-emulated view (blank page except the uncontrolled-copy notice). Full suite: 657 passed. `npx tsc --noEmit` and `eslint` clean on both changed frontend files. Ruff (check + format) clean on the changed Python files; mypy's diff against the pre-change baseline shows only the same already-tolerated `Column(...) → str | None` pattern (P5) at new line numbers in the new function, no new error category.
**Detail:** `app/api/routes/organisations.py`; `web/app/aml-program/page.tsx`; `web/lib/signup.ts`; `tests/test_p13_aml_program_document_control_smoke.py` (new).

### Stage 13 business-model pressure test — plan caps synced to the artifact, screening given a real included-quota, both add-ons actually priced and charged
**Found:** 2026-09-14, when asked "anything else need to fix this stage, ask me question, judge business model." Investigating the honest answer surfaced three real gaps rather than just opinions: the artifact's approved customer caps (50/200/500, via its comment-thread edits) had never been applied to `PLAN_CATALOGUE` or the `/pricing` page, which still enforced/advertised 100/1,000/Unlimited; "real sanctions & PEP screening" was advertised on every tier with no cost-control mechanism behind it at all; and `purchase_addon()` — confirmed by reading it, not assumed — was a pure DB write with no Stripe interaction, so the addon gating built in the previous round (Stage 13's remaining item, above) blocked unpaid access but the "purchase" endpoint it was gated behind collected no money from anyone, including for `enterprise_crypto_screening` which had a real $499/month price the whole time. **Resolved:** 2026-09-14, per your direction across three questions: screening is included up to a plan-set limit that then prompts upgrade (not pay-per-click); Quarterly Compliance Report is $220/quarter and Annual Independent Review is $1,650/year, with 20% off the review once the org has done all 3 quarters; and the artifact's plan caps are the correct ones — the code was wrong, not the memo.
**What changed:**
- **Plan caps now match what you approved in the artifact:** `PLAN_CATALOGUE` (`app/models/billing.py`) — Compliance: 100→50 customers, 2→1 seat; Scale: 1,000→200 customers; Enterprise: unlimited→500 customers (unlimited users/API calls/screening kept, matching how Enterprise already treated API calls). `/pricing` (`web/app/pricing/page.tsx`) and the `/billing` plans-tab fallback card data (`web/app/billing/page.tsx`, which was still showing a stale pre-Stage-13 $59/$79/$299 scheme even in its error-fallback path) updated to match.
- **Screening now has a real included-quota, not an unbounded promise:** added `screening_checks_month` to every plan's `limits` (Compliance 100, Scale 400, Enterprise/VVIP unlimited, free trial 20 — my own reasonable defaults sized off the new customer caps, not something you specified a number for; revisit if they're wrong). New `billing_service.enforce_screening_limit()`/`_screening_capacity()` mirror the existing `enforce_customer_limit` pattern exactly: real 403 + upgrade-prompt message, calendar-month window, counts only the screening types that actually route to a paid provider (`sanctions`/`pep`/`ubo_sanctions`/`ubo_pep` — matching `_run_real_screening`'s own real-vs-simulated split), never the simulated types or the free self-hosted DFAT/OFAC/UN `quick_screen` lookup, since neither has vendor cost to protect against. Wired into all three places a real screening check is actually created: `POST /screening/run`, `/screening/{id}/re-screen`, `POST /screening/batch`.
- **Both add-ons are now really priced and actually charge money:** `ADDON_CATALOGUE` (`app/models/billing.py`) — Quarterly Compliance Report $220/quarter, Annual Independent Review $1,650/year with a 20% bundle discount once the org has genuinely generated (not just toggled an addon for) 3 `quarterly_compliance` `BoardReport`s in the trailing 12 months — new `billing_service.addon_price()` computes this against real `BoardReport` rows, not a proxy. `purchase_addon()` now creates a real Stripe subscription (inline `price_data`, billed on the add-on's own cadence via `interval`/`interval_count` — quarterly and annual don't fit the base plan's own monthly/annual-only billing, so each add-on gets its own Stripe subscription rather than being forced onto the base one) when Stripe is configured and the org has a `stripe_customer_id`; `cancel_addon()` now cancels that Stripe subscription too. Falls back to the pre-existing DB-only toggle in mock mode or for an org with no real Stripe customer yet (e.g. an admin-granted plan that never ran Checkout) — the same fallback shape every other Stripe call in this file already uses. This generically fixed the same gap for `enterprise_crypto_screening` too, since `purchase_addon()` is shared — it was equally uncharged despite its $499/month price predating this round.
**What did NOT change / known scope limits (disclosed, not fixed this round):** the screening quota numbers (100/400/unlimited/20) are my own defaults, not numbers you gave me — there's no admin UI to edit them yet (same gap `PlanPricing` already has for base-plan prices, just not extended to limits); `quick_screen`'s free self-hosted sanctions lookup and every simulated screening type stay unmetered by design, not oversight; Stripe billing for add-ons is untestable against a real Stripe account in this environment (no `STRIPE_SECRET_KEY` here) — verified via the same mock-mode fallback path the rest of `billing_service.py`'s Stripe integration already relies on for its own tests, not against a live Stripe sandbox.
**Verified:** 21 new tests (`tests/test_p13_screening_limit_and_addon_pricing_smoke.py`) — screening quota allows-within-limit/blocks-once-exceeded/ignores-prior-month/unlimited-on-Enterprise; add-on catalogue pricing; independent review discount at 0/2/3 qualifying quarterly reports and that reports older than a year don't count; both add-ons purchase at the right (and, for the review, correctly discounted) price. Updated `tests/test_billing_addons.py`'s now-stale "`monthly_aud` is None — price TBA" assertion to check the real `price_aud`/`billing_interval` fields instead. Full suite: 668 passed. `npx tsc --noEmit` clean on the whole `web/` app; `eslint` clean on both changed frontend files (one pre-existing, unrelated `react-hooks/exhaustive-deps` warning on `billing/page.tsx` confirmed via `git stash` to predate this change). Ruff (check + format) clean on all changed/added Python files (two pre-existing, unrelated findings elsewhere in `screening.py`/`test_billing_addons.py` confirmed via `git stash` to predate this change too). mypy's diff against the pre-change baseline is byte-for-byte identical — zero new errors of any kind, not even the usual `Column(...) → X | None` noise, across all three changed backend files.
**Detail:** `app/models/billing.py`; `app/services/billing_service.py`; `app/api/routes/screening.py`; `web/app/pricing/page.tsx`; `web/app/billing/page.tsx`; `tests/test_billing_addons.py`; `tests/test_p13_screening_limit_and_addon_pricing_smoke.py` (new).

### DPMS sector rewrite (P21) — the last of the 8 sectors brought to full depth, plus a real risk-library wiring bug found along the way
**Parked:** originally as part of the 8-sector industry-content-rewrite batch (Section B) — DPMS had only received the actively-wrong cash/TTR instruction fix, not the full rewrite the other 7 sectors got. **Resolved:** 2026-09-14, per your direction ("dpms") after you picked it as the next item off the roadmap.
**What changed:**
- **Read the full real VERIGO_DPMS_* document suite from Google Drive** — TMP_Guideline, Risk_Matrix (xlsx), Sanctions_Policy, SMR_Guideline, ECDD_Guideline, KYC_Guideline, and the RATP_Addendum (Module M-10) — the same 7-document pattern already used for the other sectors, not previously read for DPMS beyond the TMP Guideline's first 8 rules.
- **`app/templates/aml/industries/dpms.py` rewritten from 156 to ~500 lines**, now overriding scope, designated_services, ewra_summary, all four risk-factor sections (customer/product/channel/geographic — proliferation financing is flagged as the highest of any Tranche 2 sector, since gold/silver/platinum/diamonds are directly PF-usable and DPRK specifically uses gold exports to fund its weapons programme), cdd_individuals, cdd_enhanced_procedures, ongoing_cdd, beneficial_ownership_procedures, pep_procedures, sanctions_procedures, transaction_monitoring, smr_procedures, ttr_procedures, employee_due_diligence, training_program_summary, record_keeping, and independent_review — closing the gap between DPMS's previous ~8-section coverage and the ~20-section depth every other rewritten sector already has. The TMP rule set grew from the previous partial DPMS-01–08 coverage to the full 22 rules (GEN-01–10 general rules plus DPMS-01–12, including the previously-missing DPMS-11 gemstone/luxury-watch rule and DPMS-12 PEP-specific rule).
- **New `app/templates/risk/industries/dpms.py`** — a 20-factor `RiskLibrary` built directly from the real `VERIGO_DPMS_Risk_Matrix_v1.xlsx` (ISO 31000:2018, 5×5), using the matrix's own `ref` IDs (CT-*, CR-*, PR-*, SG-*, DC-*, ER-*, PC-*). Category folding follows the exact precedent set for Legal/Conveyancers/Remittance/VASP: Cash Transaction Risk → transaction, Product/Service Risk → product, Sanctions/Geographic Risk → geographic, Delivery Channel Risk → channel, Employee/Internal Risk → service, Program/Compliance Risk → regulatory. Three risks tie for the matrix's highest inherent score (25/25, CRITICAL): CT-01 (cash ≥ AUD $10,000), CT-02 (structuring), and PR-01 (gold bullion as a product).
- **Found and fixed a real, independent wiring bug while building the risk library**: `app/templates/risk/factory.py`'s `INDUSTRY_MODULE_MAP` mapped both `bullion_dealers` and `precious_metals` to the generic `other` risk library — the AML side (`aml/factory.py`) was already correctly wired to `dpms` (from the earlier TTR fix), but nothing had ever pointed the risk side at a dedicated module, because no dedicated risk module existed until now. Every DPMS org's Risk Assessment page has been showing generic fallback factors, never the sector's own real 20-row register, since the day risk-framework seeding was wired up. Fixed by adding the module and repointing both industry-type entries to it.
**What did NOT change / known scope limits (disclosed, not fixed this round):** the KYC Guideline's generic company/trust/partnership/government-body CDD content (ASIC search, ABN lookup, trust deed, etc.) wasn't meaningfully different from `AMLTemplateBase`'s existing defaults, so — following the same precedent Legal set — those fields were left as inherited defaults rather than force-overridden with a re-paraphrase of the same generic content; only the fields with genuinely DPMS-specific real content were overridden.
**Verified:** the pre-existing `tests/test_dpms_ttr_template_fix_smoke.py` still passes with one assertion moved from `ttr_procedures` to the now much fuller `sanctions_procedures` (sanctioned-metals content legitimately relocated there during the rewrite, not lost — updated the test to check its new home rather than forcing stale content back into `ttr_procedures`). 2 new tests (`tests/test_dpms_risk_library_wiring_smoke.py`) confirm both `precious_metals` and `bullion_dealers` orgs now seed all 20 real risk factors (`CT-01`, `PR-01`, `SG-01`, etc.), not the generic library. Full suite: 670 passed. Ruff (check + format) clean on all changed/added files. mypy's diff against the pre-change baseline is byte-for-byte identical across all three changed/added files — the handful of errors present are pre-existing and unrelated (an `IndustryType | None` narrowing issue in both factory modules, and a `RiskCategory | None` assignment in `risk/factory.py`, none touched by this change).
**Detail:** `app/templates/aml/industries/dpms.py`; `app/templates/risk/industries/dpms.py` (new); `app/templates/risk/factory.py`; `tests/test_dpms_ttr_template_fix_smoke.py`; `tests/test_dpms_risk_library_wiring_smoke.py` (new).

### P10 — Customer-level risk engine's dimension weights are now genuinely org-configurable, plus a real multi-tenant onboarding bug found and fixed along the way
**Parked:** originally in "Parked from Stage 7," as a feature gap needing a design decision (where per-org weight overrides should live) rather than a mechanical fix. **Resolved:** 2026-09-15, per your direction to pick it up next once Stage 14 hit its Dow Jones blocker.
**What changed:**
- **Reused the org's existing, already-customisable EWRA framework instead of inventing a third weight-storage mechanism.** New `get_org_risk_weights(db, org_id)` (`app/services/customer_risk_engine.py`) reads the org's `RiskFramework.category_weights` (seeded per-industry at onboarding by `risk/factory.py`, and separately user-editable via `PATCH /risk/framework/category-weights` — the same real, working system P21's DPMS risk library slots into). The EWRA has 7 categories; this engine only scores 5 (customer/product/geographic/channel/transaction), so the relevant 5 are re-normalised to sum to 1.0 after dropping service/regulatory, falling back to the flat `DEFAULT_WEIGHTS` if an org somehow has no framework yet. Wired into both real call sites that previously never passed `weights=` at all: `POST /customers/{id}/workflow/assess-risk` and `POST /customers/{id}/rescore`.
- **Found and fixed a real multi-tenant bug while writing the cross-industry test for this**: `customers.customer_ref` carried a *global* unique index, but `_next_customer_ref()` generates it per-org (counts only that org's existing customers) — so any two different organisations onboarding their Nth customer of the same calendar year generate the identical ref (e.g. both orgs' first customer ever both get `KYC-2026-00001`), and the second org's insert was rejected outright. This would have broken customer onboarding in production for every organisation after the first to reach a given per-org sequence number in a given year. Fixed by scoping the uniqueness to `(org_id, customer_ref)` — a new composite `UniqueConstraint` on `Customer` (`app/models/customer.py`) plus Alembic migration `410a0ddbee2b`, actually applied against a real local Postgres 16 instance (started for this purpose, not assumed) to verify: the old global unique *index* (not a named constraint — `ix_customers_customer_ref`, confirmed by inspecting the real schema rather than guessing from the model declaration) drops and recreates correctly; the composite constraint applies correctly against an already-migrated database; the fresh-database guard (baseline `create_all()` already producing the target state) correctly no-ops; and the downgrade path restores the exact original schema. Postgres instance and test database torn down afterward.
**What did NOT change / known scope limits (disclosed, not fixed this round):** there's still no UI for editing the 5-dimension weights directly — they're only configurable indirectly via the EWRA's `PATCH /risk/framework/category-weights` (which does have a UI), since that's the one real per-org weight-editing surface that already exists; a dedicated 5-dimension-only editing UI wasn't requested and would just duplicate that surface.
**Verified:** 4 new tests (`tests/test_customer_risk_engine_org_weights_smoke.py`) — no-framework fallback, an org's weights differing from the flat default, two different industries producing genuinely different weights (DPMS weights transaction risk highest, matching its real cash/structuring-heavy Risk Matrix; Legal weights customer risk highest, matching its real CDD-refusal-heavy one), and an end-to-end proof that `POST /customers/{id}/rescore` now produces different scores for identical customer inputs across two different-industry orgs — before this fix that assertion would have failed, since both orgs scored on the same flat weights. Full suite: 674 passed. Ruff (check + format, using CI's actual pinned-at-runtime version, not just the older local venv one) clean. mypy's diff against the pre-change baseline: 3 new errors surfaced in the new function itself (nullable JSON-column and `RiskCategoryType | None` typing) — these were new code, not legacy debt, so fixed properly (explicit `float()` coercion, filtering `None` category types) rather than tolerated; zero errors remain in the diff.
**Detail:** `app/services/customer_risk_engine.py`; `app/api/routes/customers.py`; `app/api/routes/customer_workflow.py`; `app/models/customer.py`; `alembic/versions/410a0ddbee2b_scope_customer_ref_uniqueness_to_org.py` (new); `tests/test_customer_risk_engine_org_weights_smoke.py` (new).

## Stage 15 (Security Hardening) — first pass, 2026-09-15

**Scope:** a real security-posture survey across auth/session, authorization, secrets, transport/headers, input validation, rate limiting, audit logging, and dependency/data-at-rest — not assumed from filenames, each area read and confirmed. Most of what a "hardening" pass might target turned out already solid (prior work in this codebase is headed "SECURITY-HARDENED" / "Zero Trust hardening" in several files' own docstrings): bcrypt password hashing with timing-attack-safe dummy-verify, JWT revocation via a Redis-backed blacklist, MFA, consistent org-scoped IDOR protection across every route file checked, no hardcoded secrets, a production/staging CORS+secret-key guard, full security-headers middleware (HSTS/CSP/X-Frame-Options/etc.), no raw-SQL injection surface, real file-upload magic-byte + path-traversal protection, tiered IP-based rate limiting, and clean audit logging with no credential leakage. Seven concrete gaps came out of that survey; the four below were fixed this round, the other three are parked with a stated reason rather than forced through.

### Fixed this round
- **Account lockout was schema-only, never wired up.** `User.failed_login_count`/`locked_until` have existed on the model the whole time, but nothing ever read or wrote them — `authenticate_user()` only checked password + status, so `RateLimitMiddleware`'s login throttle (per-IP, 10/min) was the *only* brute-force defence; a distributed attack (few attempts per account, from many IPs) was never slowed at all. Wired the existing columns in: `authenticate_user()` (`app/services/auth_service.py`) now locks an account for 15 minutes after 5 wrong passwords in a row, resets the counter on a genuine success, and a locked account gets a distinct `423` with a "try again in N minutes" message from `POST /auth/login` rather than being silently treated as one more wrong-password 401.
- **Registration had no password length/complexity check at all.** `/me/change-password` and `/password-reset/confirm` both already reject anything under 12 characters; `/auth/register` (`app/api/routes/auth.py`) never did, so a self-registered account could set a 1-character password. Fixed with the same 12-char minimum, for consistency with the two endpoints that already enforce it.
- **The production-secrets guard didn't cover staging.** `Settings.check_production_secrets()` (`app/config.py`) already rejects wildcard CORS in both `production` and `staging` (staging is just as internet-exposed, and `CORSMiddleware` runs with `allow_credentials=True` regardless of environment) — but the adjacent check for the default `SECRET_KEY` value only fired for `environment == "production"`. A staging deploy left at the source-default secret would sign valid auth tokens using a value visible in the public repo. Extended the same check to staging.
- **Dependency vulnerabilities, real ones, not just missing tooling.** There was no vulnerability scanning of any kind in CI. Ran `pip-audit`/`npm audit` for real rather than just adding an empty step: found and fixed `python-multipart` 0.0.9→0.0.32 (multiple CVEs, request-smuggling/DoS class), `python-jose[cryptography]` 3.3.0→3.5.0 (JWT algorithm-confusion CVEs — this is the library issuing every session token in the app) which also let its transitive `pyasn1` pin loosen and resolve to a patched 0.6.4, and frontend `postcss` 8.4.38→8.5.28 (XSS/path-traversal CVEs in its CSS stringifier) — `web/package.json`'s pin bumped directly since it's a top-level devDependency, not just transitive. Added `pip-audit` (backend) and `npm audit --omit=dev` (frontend) as blocking CI steps (`.github/workflows/ci.yml`) so a newly-disclosed CVE in a direct dependency fails the build going forward, not silently ships.
**What did NOT change / known scope limits (disclosed, not fixed this round — three genuinely parked, not forgotten):**
- **`ecdsa` 0.19.2 (PYSEC-2026-1325)** — a transitive dependency `python-jose` pulls in regardless of the `[cryptography]` extra, with no fix version published (the python-ecdsa maintainers have stated they won't fully close this class of pure-Python timing side-channel). Confirmed this app only ever signs/verifies with HS256 (`app/services/auth_service.py`), never an ECDSA-family algorithm, so the vulnerable code path is loaded but never exercised. Ignored explicitly (with this reasoning inline) in the new CI `pip-audit` step rather than left to fail the build forever.
- **`pytest` 8.2.0 (PYSEC-2026-1845)** — dev-only, no production exposure. The fix needs a major `pytest` 8→9 bump, which in turn needs `pytest-asyncio` 0.23→1.x (a breaking major version with different default-mode semantics) and `pytest-cov` bumped too — real risk of quietly breaking async test fixtures across 681 tests for a tool that never ships. Deserves its own dedicated pass with a full regression run, not a same-round bundle-in. Ignored in CI with the reasoning inline, same as `ecdsa`.
- **`starlette` 0.37.2 (7 CVEs, several DoS-class)** — this one *is* production-exposed (the actual ASGI layer under every request `fastapi` serves), so higher-priority than the two above, but fixing it isn't a `starlette`-alone bump: `fastapi==0.111.0` pins it, and jumping to a `fastapi` version that pulls a patched `starlette` (tried `0.141.1`) immediately hit a `pydantic` conflict — it needs `pydantic` bumped off `2.7.1` too. That's a cascading three-package major-version chain touching every route/schema file in a 56-route-file app, not a one-line pin change; tried it, watched pip's resolver reject it outright, backed it out rather than force a wider bump un-asked. Ignored in CI with the reasoning inline; **this is the one of the three worth prioritising next**, given it's the only one of the three actually in the request path.
- **CSP still allows `script-src 'unsafe-inline'`** (`app/middleware.py`'s `SecurityHeadersMiddleware`, already flagged as a known TODO in its own inline comment before this round) and **`identity_number`/`tax_identification_number` on `Customer`/beneficial-owner rows have no application-level encryption** (`app/models/customer.py`) — they rely on whatever encryption-at-rest the underlying Postgres provides, unlike connector/integration credentials which already go through `app/services/crypto.py`'s Fernet layer. Both are real, both untouched this round: the CSP fix risks breaking whatever inline scripts the frontend currently relies on without an actual audit of every page, and field-level encryption of `id_number` is a genuine schema-plus-migration-plus-key-management decision (and would need a strategy for existing plaintext rows) — not something to guess at for exactly the KYC-identity-number field class this session was just told to leave alone pending your offline review.
**Verified:** 7 new tests — `tests/test_stage15_login_hardening_smoke.py` (5): lockout after 5 failed attempts blocks even the correct password with 423, successful login resets the counter, `authenticate_user()` enforces the lock directly, registration rejects <12-char passwords, accepts >=12; `tests/test_cors_wildcard_staging_smoke.py` gained 2 more: default secret rejected in staging, allowed in development. Full suite: 681 passed (674 before this round + these 7 new), no regressions from any of the dependency bumps. `npm run build` clean with `postcss` bumped. Ruff (check + format, CI's pinned version) clean. mypy: zero errors project-wide, same as before this round.
**Detail:** `app/services/auth_service.py`; `app/api/routes/auth.py`; `app/config.py`; `requirements.txt`; `web/package.json`; `web/package-lock.json`; `.github/workflows/ci.yml`; `tests/test_stage15_login_hardening_smoke.py` (new); `tests/test_cors_wildcard_staging_smoke.py`.

## Stage 15 (Security Hardening) — second pass, 2026-09-15 (P48, P50)

Picked up two of the three items parked from the first pass, per your direction ("Both").

### P48 — `starlette`'s 7 CVEs fixed via the `fastapi`+`pydantic` bump the first pass had backed out of
**Parked:** first pass, same day — `fastapi==0.141.1` needs `pydantic>=2.9.0`, and pip's resolver rejected the bump outright against the then-current `pydantic==2.7.1` pin.
**What changed:** `requirements.txt` — `fastapi` 0.111.0→0.141.1, `pydantic` 2.7.1→2.13.5, `pydantic-settings` 2.2.1→2.15.0 (kept in step with `pydantic`, since it depends on it). `starlette` resolves transitively to 1.6.0, well past every CVE fix version pip-audit had flagged. One real new mypy error surfaced from this, not tolerated: `RateLimitMiddleware.__init__` (`app/middleware.py`) typed its `app` parameter as the concrete `FastAPI` class, which starlette's newer, stricter `_MiddlewareFactory` protocol no longer accepts (every sibling middleware class in the same file relies on `BaseHTTPMiddleware`'s own already-correctly-typed `__init__` instead of overriding it) — retyped to `starlette.types.ASGIApp`, matching what `BaseHTTPMiddleware` itself expects and what the other middleware classes get for free.
**What did NOT change / known scope limits:** `uvicorn` stays at 0.29.0 — not implicated in any flagged CVE, left untouched to keep this bump scoped to what P48 needed. The `class Config:`-style Pydantic v1 config blocks already flagged as deprecated (mypy noted this pre-existing pattern in the first pass too) are unaffected — pydantic 2.13 still supports them, removal is a v3 concern, not this bump's.
**Verified:** full suite 681 passed, no regressions (same count as before this round — this is a dependency/type-fix change, not new test surface). mypy: zero errors (the one new error from the `ASGIApp` typing gap is fixed, not tolerated). Ruff (check + format, CI's pinned version) clean. pip-audit: the 7 starlette CVE IDs are gone from its output entirely — only `pytest` (P49, still parked) and `ecdsa` (no fix exists) remain, both already disclosed/ignored in CI. CI's `pip-audit` step's ignore list trimmed from 9 IDs down to 2 to match.
**Detail:** `requirements.txt`; `app/middleware.py`; `.github/workflows/ci.yml`.

### P50 — real CSP + HSTS added to the frontend origin, not the backend's
**Parked:** first pass, framed as "CSP still allows `script-src 'unsafe-inline'`" — that framing was wrong. What actually has `unsafe-inline` is `app/middleware.py`'s `SecurityHeadersMiddleware`, which only wraps the FastAPI **backend's JSON API responses**. The page a browser actually loads (this Next.js app, served by Vercel) had **no CSP at all** — `web/vercel.json` only ever set `X-Content-Type-Options`/`X-Frame-Options`/`Referrer-Policy`, and `web/next.config.js`'s `headers()` only covered `/sw.js`/`/manifest.json` cache headers. That's the real gap this item needed to close.
**What changed:** `web/next.config.js`'s `headers()` now sets a real `Content-Security-Policy` and `Strict-Transport-Security` on every route (`'/(.*)'`), built from what the app actually loads (audited, not assumed): `connect-src` allows `self` + `NEXT_PUBLIC_API_URL` (read at build time, so it tracks whichever backend origin that deployment actually points at, not a hardcoded `api.verigo.com.au`) + Sentry's documented ingest domains (the exact one depends on `NEXT_PUBLIC_SENTRY_DSN`, not present in this repo, so allowed by pattern rather than guessed at one project subdomain); `style-src`/`font-src` cover the Google Fonts `<link>` tags actually in `app/layout.tsx`; `frame-ancestors 'none'`, `object-src 'none'`, `base-uri 'self'`, `form-action 'self'`, `upgrade-insecure-requests` added as real, no-cost hardening. Verified end-to-end, not just written and assumed correct: built and started the production server, confirmed the headers on a live response, then loaded the homepage, `/pricing` (static marketing), `/dashboard` (app shell) and `/solutions/vasp` (an SSG industry page) in a real headless Chromium and watched the console for CSP violations — none on any of the four.
**What did NOT change / a real tradeoff found and disclosed, not silently decided:** `script-src` keeps `'unsafe-inline'` — tried the alternative first. A nonce-based `script-src` (dropping `unsafe-inline` entirely) needs the nonce read via `next/headers`' `headers()` inside a Server Component, since `next-themes`' `ThemeProvider` (the one inline `<script>` this app renders itself, to set the theme class before paint and avoid a flash of the wrong theme) takes a `nonce` prop for exactly this. Built it — middleware generating a per-request nonce, `app/layout.tsx` reading it via `headers()` and passing it to `ThemeProvider` — and rebuilt: every marketing and app page that was static (`○`) or SSG'd (`●`) became dynamic (`ƒ`), not just the 3 routes that already were. That's the whole site (60+ routes) losing CDN caching, a real site-wide performance/cost cost, not a one-line fix, so it was backed out rather than shipped silently. Tracked as its own item (P50b) below rather than folded into this one, since it's a genuine build/traffic-cost decision, not a security correctness question.
**Verified:** `npx tsc --noEmit` clean, `npm run lint` clean (0 errors; the pre-existing warnings unrelated to this change), `npm run build` — confirmed byte-for-byte identical static/SSG route breakdown to before this change (same routes marked `○`/`●`/`ƒ`), headers present in `.next/routes-manifest.json` and on a live `npm run start` response, zero CSP console violations across 4 representative pages in headless Chromium (homepage, static marketing, app shell, SSG industry page).
**Detail:** `web/next.config.js`.

### P50b — nonce-based `script-src` (dropping `unsafe-inline` on the frontend) costs full-site static generation
**Resolved on your direction, 2026-09-15 ("use nonce")** — see "Stage 16 — fifth pass" below for the full detail. Originally parked here because it's a real traffic-cost/performance decision, not a security-correctness one — you were asked with the concrete tradeoff spelled out and chose to accept it.
**What it was:** Removing `'unsafe-inline'` from the frontend's `script-src` for real (not just formally) requires Next.js's documented per-request-nonce pattern (middleware/proxy + `headers()` in the root layout), which forces every page under that layout into dynamic, per-request rendering — confirmed by actually building it, not assumed from documentation alone. This app has ~60 marketing/app routes statically or SSG-prerendered; all of them lose that.
**Detail:** the first (reverted) prototype is described in P50's own "what did NOT change" note above; the shipped version is in "Stage 16 — fifth pass" below.

CI's `pip-audit` ignore list trimmed from 2 IDs to 1 (`ecdsa` only — the one genuinely unfixable finding).

Ruff (check + format, CI's pinned version) clean. pip-audit: `pytest`'s CVE ID is gone from its output; only `ecdsa` (no fix exists upstream) remains, already disclosed. `npm run build` confirmed unaffected (backend-only change).

## P36 — raw ORM responses silently returning `{}`, root-caused and fixed, 2026-09-15

**Parked:** originally in section F, found while writing P33's audit-trail regression test — confirmed the bug reproduced but never isolated why, out of scope for that round. **Resolved:** per your direction, picked up as the one item worth fixing before scoping Stage 16 (Testing), since walking into a testing-focused stage with a known, unexplained "endpoint silently returns empty data" bug undermines the premise of that stage.
**Root cause, confirmed by reproduction, not assumed:** five endpoints in `risk_assessment.py` construct/update an ORM object, `db.commit()`, `db.refresh(x)`, then call the shared `_log()` audit helper — which issues its *own* `db.commit()`. SQLAlchemy's default `expire_on_commit=True` means that second commit expires every attribute on `x` again. None of these five routes declare a `response_model`, so FastAPI serialises the raw ORM object via `jsonable_encoder`'s fallback path, which reads `vars(x)`/`x.__dict__` directly — unlike normal attribute access (`x.name`), this never triggers SQLAlchemy's instrumented-attribute lazy-reload, so an expired object serialises as an empty object. Reproduced live via a real HTTP test against the unmodified code (confirmed `resp.json() == {}`), then confirmed the same 4 new regression tests fail against the pre-fix code and pass against the fix (not just written to pass), via `git stash`.
**Which endpoints were actually affected — audited, not assumed:** grepped every route file for the same `db.commit()` → `db.refresh(x)` → `_log()`/`audit_service.log_action()` → `return x` shape (36 matches across 11 files), then checked each one's *actual* `@router` decorator (including multi-line ones, which a first pass of the check missed and had to be redone) for `response_model=`. All but 5 already declare one — pydantic's `from_attributes` validation reads each field via `getattr()`, which does trigger SQLAlchemy's reload, so those were never actually at risk despite sharing the same code shape. Only the 5 in `risk_assessment.py` (none of which have a `response_model`) were real: `add_custom_factor()`, `add_mitigation()`, `update_mitigation()`, `create_mitigation_library_item()`, `update_mitigation_library_item()`.
**What changed:** all 5 reordered so `db.refresh(x)` is the last database call before `return x` — `_log()` now runs first. `_log()`'s own attribute reads on `x` (e.g. `mit.id`) happen through normal `getattr` access, which correctly reloads any already-expired attribute from the first commit, so this reordering doesn't lose anything; the final `refresh()` then guarantees `x.__dict__` is fully populated at the moment FastAPI serialises it.
**What did NOT change / known scope limits:** no `response_model` was added to these 5 endpoints — the minimal, root-cause fix (correct ordering) was preferred over a larger schema-adding change not asked for; declaring proper response schemas for them remains a reasonable follow-up but wasn't required to fix the actual bug. The other 31 matches of the same code *shape* elsewhere in the codebase were confirmed safe (via `response_model`) rather than touched.
**Verified:** 4 new tests (`tests/test_p36_raw_orm_response_smoke.py`) covering all 5 endpoints, each asserting real field values are present in the response body (not just a 200/201 status, which is all the pre-existing P33 test checked — how this went unnoticed). Confirmed each test fails against the pre-fix code and passes against the fix via `git stash`. Full suite: 685 passed (681 + these 4). mypy: zero errors. Ruff (check + format, CI's pinned version) clean.
**Detail:** `app/api/routes/risk_assessment.py`; `tests/test_p36_raw_orm_response_smoke.py` (new).

## Stage 16 (Testing) — first pass, 2026-09-15

**Scope:** a real testing-posture survey first (not assumed): 685 backend tests, ~73% coverage, zero frontend tests, and — the finding this round acted on — CI provisions a real Postgres 16 service container that's never actually used; the whole suite runs on SQLite (`tests/conftest.py` hardcodes it), and the real Alembic migration chain has only ever been verified by manually spinning up Postgres during specific bug-fix sessions (P37, P47, P10 all found real bugs this way, per their own entries above), never as a repeatable, automated check.

### Real bugs found by actually running the suite against Postgres, not assumed
Rather than just writing a migration-chain test in isolation, stood up a real local Postgres 16 instance and ran the *entire* 685-test suite against it (via a new opt-in `TEST_DATABASE_URL` env var in `conftest.py` — deliberately not the `DATABASE_URL` name CI already sets at job level, since reading that would have silently flipped the whole suite off SQLite the moment this file changed, rather than staying an explicit opt-in). This surfaced two real, previously invisible correctness bugs, both masked completely by SQLite's much looser DDL/constraint enforcement:
- **A genuine circular foreign-key pair with no `use_alter`.** `customers.business_detail_id` → `customer_business_details.id` and `customer_business_details.customer_id` → `customers.id` reference each other. SQLAlchemy's `create_all()`/`drop_all()` (used by the test suite, and by anything else that stands up a fresh schema without going through Alembic) can't topologically sort a genuine cycle without one side marked `use_alter=True` — it raised `CircularDependencyError` the instant it ran against real Postgres. SQLite's DDL is loose enough to never hit this. Alembic's own migration chain was never affected (each `ALTER TABLE ADD CONSTRAINT` there is independent, not subject to `create_all()`'s single-pass topological sort) — confirmed by running the real migration chain against fresh Postgres before touching anything, which passed cleanly. Fixed by adding `use_alter=True` + an explicit constraint name to the `customers` side.
- **~30 test files' local helper functions insert a `User`/`Customer`/`Subscription` row with an arbitrary literal `org_id` string, with no `Organisation` row ever created at that id.** SQLite doesn't enforce `org_id`'s FK to `organisations.id` by default, so this "worked" purely by omission; Postgres correctly rejects every one of these as a `ForeignKeyViolation`. Fixed the shared root cause (`tests/conftest.py`'s `_make_user()`, used by ~30 files) by having it create a matching `Organisation` at the literal id when one doesn't already exist, rather than touching every call site individually — this alone took the Postgres failure count from 113 down to 97.
**What did NOT change / known scope limits, quantified rather than hand-waved:** the remaining **97 test failures against real Postgres** (out of 685) are the same class of bug, but scattered across each test file's *own* local one-off helper (`_make_customer`, `_upgrade`, inline `User(...)`/`Customer(...)` construction, etc.) rather than one shared function — fixing all of them is a real, distributed, multi-file effort, not a quick follow-on, and wasn't forced through this round. Sampled several categories of the 97 (governance policy seeding, billing subscriptions, cross-tenant IDOR tests, Sumsub integration) and confirmed they're all the identical root-cause pattern (a literal id referencing a row that was never created), not new distinct bugs — so this is a large but *understood*, mechanical cleanup, not an open unknown. CI's default `pytest tests/` run stays on SQLite exactly as before (confirmed 685 passed, unchanged) — the existing suite was not switched to Postgres and does not need to be for this round's fixes to have value.
**What shipped instead, safely additive:** a new `tests/test_migration_chain_smoke.py` — runs the real Alembic chain (upgrade head from empty, downgrade one step, re-upgrade) against CI's already-provisioned Postgres service (using the `DATABASE_URL` CI already sets, exactly as `alembic/env.py` does), with table/constraint spot-checks proving the whole chain actually ran rather than just exiting 0. Skips cleanly (not failing) when no real Postgres `DATABASE_URL` is configured, so a normal local `pytest tests/` run is unaffected. No CI workflow change was needed — CI's `pytest tests/` step already sets `DATABASE_URL` at job level, so this test activates automatically on the next push, in both the 3.11 and 3.12 matrix legs.
**Verified:** the 2 new migration tests pass against a real local Postgres 16 instance (confirmed both the fresh-upgrade and downgrade/re-upgrade paths); confirmed they skip cleanly with no `DATABASE_URL`. Full suite re-run with CI's exact env (`DATABASE_URL` set, matching what CI actually does) — 687 passed (685 + 2 new), proving the rest of the suite genuinely stays SQLite-only despite `DATABASE_URL` being present, not just in theory. Full suite on plain local `pytest tests/` (no Postgres at all) — 685 passed, 2 skipped. mypy: zero errors. Ruff (check + format, CI's pinned version) clean on all changed/added files. Local Postgres instance and test database torn down afterward, matching the same discipline used for the P10 migration verification earlier.
**Detail:** `app/models/customer.py`; `tests/conftest.py`; `tests/test_migration_chain_smoke.py` (new).

## Stage 16 (Testing) — second pass, 2026-09-15 (P53)

**What changed:** stood up a real frontend test stack from zero — Vitest 5 + React Testing Library for unit/component tests, Playwright for e2e, both wired into CI (`.github/workflows/ci.yml`'s frontend job; Playwright's browser install + e2e run only on the Node 20 matrix leg, to avoid doubling CI cost for a check that doesn't depend on Node version). Had to bump `@types/node` 20.12.12→22.20.2 to satisfy current Vitest/Vite's peer requirements — checked with `npx tsc --noEmit` before and after, clean either way. First real suite, not scaffolding:
- **Unit tests** (`lib/pricing.test.ts`, `lib/analyticsConsent.test.ts`): real business logic customers/visitors actually depend on — the money-formatting and annual-discount-percentage functions shown on the pricing page, and the analytics opt-out/opt-in localStorage behaviour the Australian Privacy Act disclosure (`CookieNotice`) relies on.
- **Component test** (`components/CookieNotice.test.tsx`): renders the real component, confirms first-visit visibility vs. already-dismissed suppression, and that clicking "Opt out of analytics" vs. "Got it" actually writes (or doesn't write) the real localStorage key `setAnalyticsOptOut()` checks.
- **E2E tests** (`e2e/marketing-pages.spec.ts`): a real Chromium browser loading the real built app (`next build && next start`) — homepage and pricing page render their actual headings with no console errors, an SSG industry page returns 200, and two tests that directly regression-test this session's own earlier P50 work: the homepage response actually carries the real `Content-Security-Policy`/`Strict-Transport-Security` headers, and loading it produces zero CSP violations in a real browser (this exact check was done manually via a throwaway Playwright script during P50 — now it's permanent, automated, and runs in CI).
**What did NOT change / known scope limits:** this is a first real suite proving the stack works end-to-end, not coverage of all 62 routes — that remains open-ended future work, intentionally not re-tracked as its own parking-lot ID (adding tests as routes change is now just normal engineering, not a special project). No auth-flow/backend-dependent e2e tests were written (login, onboarding, billing checkout) since they need a live backend this environment doesn't have running by default; the marketing/static pages tested don't have that dependency. `@playwright/test`'s pinned version (1.63.0) doesn't match this sandbox's pre-installed Chromium build exactly — `playwright.config.ts` uses the pre-installed browser directly when present (this dev sandbox) and falls back to Playwright's own resolution otherwise (CI, a real dev machine), checked via `fs.existsSync` rather than assuming one environment.
**Verified:** all 16 unit/component tests and all 5 e2e tests pass locally (the e2e run's one environment-specific console message — a Google Fonts request failing TLS validation against this sandbox's own outbound-proxy CA, `net::ERR_CERT_AUTHORITY_INVALID` — is explicitly filtered as sandbox noise, not an app error, with the reasoning left in a comment rather than silently loosening the assertion). `npx tsc --noEmit` clean, `npm run lint` clean (0 errors, same pre-existing warnings as before), `npm run build`'s static/SSG route breakdown unaffected by the new test files (Next.js correctly ignores them). `npm audit`: 0 vulnerabilities on the new devDependencies.
**Detail:** `web/package.json`; `web/vitest.config.mts` (new); `web/vitest.setup.ts` (new); `web/playwright.config.ts` (new); `web/lib/pricing.test.ts` (new); `web/lib/analyticsConsent.test.ts` (new); `web/components/CookieNotice.test.tsx` (new); `web/e2e/marketing-pages.spec.ts` (new); `.github/workflows/ci.yml`; `.gitignore`.

### Same-day CI fix — the latest Vitest/jsdom versions don't actually run on CI's Node 20 leg
**Found:** real CI failure on `Frontend (Node 20)` right after the push above — passed everywhere locally because this sandbox happens to run Node v22.22.2, which coincidentally satisfies jsdom 30's exact minimum. GitHub's Node 20 runner does not.
**Root cause, checked precisely rather than guessed:** `jsdom@30`'s own `engines` field is `^22.22.2 || ^24.15.0 || >=26.0.0` — it does not support Node 20 *at all*, and its bundled `undici` crashes at import (`TypeError: webidl.util.markAsUncloneable is not a function`) on any Node without that specific internal API. Separately, `vitest@5.0.1`'s own `engines` field is `^22.12.0 || ^24.0.0 || >=26.0.0` — also no Node 20 support. Both packages were installed at "latest" without checking their actual engines constraints against CI's own Node 20/22 matrix.
**Fix:** downgraded to versions with broad Node 18/20/22 compatibility, checked via `npm view <pkg> engines` before installing rather than assumed: `vitest` 5.0.1→3.2.7, `jsdom` 30.0.1→26.1.0, `@testing-library/jest-dom` 7.0.1→6.9.1 (7.x requires Node >=22 too), `@vitejs/plugin-react` 6.1.1→4.7.0 (6.x needs a Vite version vitest 3 doesn't use). Tried `vitest@4.1.11` first (also Node-20-compatible per its engines field) but its new optional-peer-dependency tree (`@vitest/browser-playwright`, pulling in `canvas`/`msw`) triggers a genuine npm 10.9.7 `arborist` internal crash (`Cannot read properties of null (reading 'edgesOut')`) on a clean install — a real npm tooling bug, not a version-pinning mistake; reverted to 3.2.7 rather than fight it.
**What did NOT change / known scope limit, disclosed not hidden:** this leaves one moderate, dev-only CVE unresolved -- `@vitest/mocker`'s path-traversal/arbitrary-file-read advisory (GHSA-82fw-gwwq-j7x9), fixed only in `vitest>=5.0.1`, which (as above) doesn't run on CI's Node 20 leg. Confirmed CI's actual `npm audit --omit=dev --audit-level=high` step only checks production dependencies and reports 0 vulnerabilities regardless — this finding never reaches CI's gate — same disclosed-and-accepted category as the backend's `ecdsa` finding (P-series dependency work, Stage 15).
**Verified:** all 16 unit/component tests and all 5 e2e tests pass on a from-scratch reinstall (`rm -rf node_modules package-lock.json && npm install`, not just `npm install` on top of a possibly-stale lock). Ran the e2e suite 5 times in a row after this fix to specifically check for flakiness (one earlier run had flaked on a sandbox-only network artifact — a Google Fonts request rejected by this environment's own egress-proxy policy, manifesting as either a TLS or a 404-shaped console message depending on timing; broadened the e2e tests' error filter to exclude resource-load failures generically, not just the one exact message shape first seen) — 5/5 clean. `npx tsc --noEmit`, `npm run lint` (0 errors), `npm run build` all clean.
**Detail:** `web/package.json`; `web/package-lock.json`; `web/e2e/marketing-pages.spec.ts`.

## Stage 16 (Testing) — third pass, 2026-09-15 (P52)

**Scope:** fixed all 97 test failures the first pass's Postgres run left open, catalogued as P52. Re-ran the full suite against a fresh local Postgres 16 instance to confirm the count (97, matching the earlier estimate exactly), then fixed each of the 11 affected files' own local fixture helper — 24 unique test functions once parametrization is collapsed, not 97 independent cases.

**What changed, by root cause:**
- **Literal FK-referencing IDs with no backing row (the anticipated pattern, most of the 97):** `tests/test_coverage_boost.py` (76 failures — `seed_aml_solution`/`seed_risk_framework`/`seed_questionnaire_for_org` tests passed literal `created_by="user_1"`, `solution_id="sol_1"`, `"org-q1"` strings that a production caller would never actually pass; fixed by creating real `User`/`AMLSolution`/`Organisation` rows via the existing `_make_user`/`_make_org` conftest helpers, matching how the real callers in `app/templates/aml/factory.py` and `app/templates/risk/factory.py` actually invoke these functions), `tests/test_p13_screening_limit_and_addon_pricing_smoke.py` (10 — `_upgrade()`'s literal `org_id` now auto-creates the matching `Organisation`; `_add_screening_records()` now also creates the `Customer` row `ScreeningRecord.customer_id` FKs to), `tests/test_critical14_idor_round2_smoke.py` (1 — `_make_run()`/`_make_history_row()` now build the real `RiskFramework → RiskCategory → RiskFactor → RiskFactorScore` chain instead of passing opaque literal ids), `tests/test_subscription_unique_constraint_smoke.py` (1 — literal `organisation_id="12345"` now backed by a real `Organisation`), `tests/test_sumsub_integration.py` (1 — `_make_customer()` now auto-creates the org, same pattern as conftest's `_make_user()`).
- **A genuine production bug, found only because this pass went past the anticipated pattern to actually read every traceback rather than assuming they were all the same shape:** `app/services/monitoring_engine.py`'s `run_monitoring()` creates a `TransactionAlert` then, a few lines later, `RegulatoryRecommendation` rows referencing it by `alert_id` — but `RegulatoryRecommendation.alert_id` is a bare `Column(ForeignKey(...))` with no ORM `relationship()` to `TransactionAlert`. SQLAlchemy's unit-of-work insert ordering is driven by `relationship()`-derived dependency edges, not raw FK columns, so without one it has no way to know the alert row must be inserted first — on SQLite (FK enforcement off) the wrong order never mattered; on Postgres it's a real `ForeignKeyViolation`, and in production this is the same silent risk (order not guaranteed) even though SQLite masked it in every prior test run. Fixed with a single `db.flush()` between the alert-creation loop and the recommendation-generation call — `alert.id` is already assigned client-side, so this just forces the row to actually exist in the DB before anything references it. This one fix alone cleared `test_alert_create_case_smoke.py` (2) and, transitively, 3 more files that go through the same real transaction→monitoring→alert pipeline: `test_stage8_transaction_creation_triggers_monitoring_smoke.py` (1), `test_stage9_case_audit_trail_and_alerts_readback_smoke.py` (1), `test_monitoring_dedup_smoke.py` (1), `test_stage8_default_monitoring_rules_seeded_smoke.py` (1) — none of these files' own test code needed any change.
- **A second genuine production bug:** `organisations.abn` was `String(11)` — exactly an ABN's raw digit count — but the org-settings PATCH endpoint accepts and round-trips the human-formatted value as the user typed it ("XX XXX XXX XXX", 14 chars with spaces), confirmed by `test_onboarding_wizard.py`'s own assertion that the spaced format comes back unchanged. SQLite doesn't enforce `VARCHAR` length so this silently truncated-or-passed there; Postgres correctly rejected the insert (`StringDataRightTruncation`). Fixed by widening the column to `String(20)` (`app/models/organisation.py`) plus a new Alembic migration (`82cdd805f838_widen_organisation_abn_column.py`) so real Postgres deployments actually get the wider column, not just the test suite's own `create_all()`.

**What did NOT change:** no test file's actual assertions were altered — every fix was either backing a literal id with a real row, or (for the 2 production bugs) a minimal root-cause fix in application code. `tests/conftest.py`'s already-fixed `_make_user()` needed no further change. CI's default `pytest tests/` run stays on SQLite, unaffected by any of this.

**Verified:** full suite against a real local Postgres 16 instance — 685 passed, 2 skipped, 0 failed (down from 97 failed at the start of this pass). Full suite against default SQLite — also 685 passed, 2 skipped, confirming no regression on CI's actual gate. Coverage re-run with CI's exact flags (`--cov-fail-under=58`) — 72.88%, gate untouched. `tests/test_migration_chain_smoke.py` (upgrade-from-empty + downgrade/re-upgrade) re-run against Postgres to confirm the new `abn` migration chains cleanly — both pass. mypy: zero errors. Ruff (check + format, `app/` — CI's actual lint scope, which does not cover `tests/`) clean.
**Detail:** `app/services/monitoring_engine.py`; `app/models/organisation.py`; `alembic/versions/82cdd805f838_widen_organisation_abn_column.py` (new); `tests/test_coverage_boost.py`; `tests/test_p13_screening_limit_and_addon_pricing_smoke.py`; `tests/test_critical14_idor_round2_smoke.py`; `tests/test_subscription_unique_constraint_smoke.py`; `tests/test_sumsub_integration.py`.

## Stage 16 (Testing) — fourth pass, 2026-09-15 (P54, first module)

**Scope:** started P54 (the 4 real backend modules under 35% covered) by picking the highest real-usage/risk item first rather than the lowest raw percentage. Checked actual call sites, not just coverage %, for each of the 4 named modules: `automation_engine.py` is `import`ed by 6 route files (`transactions.py`, `customers.py`, `customer_workflow.py`, `screening.py`, `kyc.py`, `independent_review.py`, plus `rule_builder.py` itself and `notification_scheduler.py`) and fires on nearly every core customer/transaction/screening workflow action; `governance/training.py` and `dashboard.py` are large but single-route-file surfaces; `risk_triggered_training_service.py` is used only by `training_triggers.py`. Widest blast radius wins — started there.

**What changed:** new `tests/test_automation_engine.py` (49 tests), two layers matching how the module is actually used:
- **Request-level, through the real API:** create a rule via `POST /rule-builder/rules`, trigger it via a real `POST /transactions/`, and check the real side effect (a `Task` row existing) rather than just a 200 status — covers the matching case, the non-matching case (condition doesn't fire → no side effect), and shadow mode (`status=testing` → conditions evaluate and log `would_execute_shadow_mode`, but the real DB is never touched, checked directly rather than trusting the execution log's own self-report).
- **Direct service-level, one real DB side effect per action type:** the condition evaluator (`evaluate_condition_groups`) exercised per-operator (`eq`/`ne`/`gt`/`gte`/`lt`/`lte`/`in`/`not_in`/`contains`/`starts_with`/`is_true`/`is_false`/`is_null`/`between`), dotted field-path resolution, per-leaf and per-group `negate`, nested AND/OR sub-groups, and the "top-level groups are OR'd, empty group always matches, empty rule list never matches" semantics from the module's own docstring; then each action handler in the dispatch table (`create_alert`, `create_case`, `create_task`/`request_document`, `schedule_review`, `create_calendar_item`, `set_risk_level`, `flag_smr_candidate`, `escalate_mlro`, `send_email`, `trigger_webhook` incl. its `httpx.HTTPError` branch, the `manual_action_required` no-op actions), plus the entry point's own bookkeeping (`AutomationRuleExecution` written whether matched or not, `trigger_count` increments only on match, inactive rules never evaluated, an action's exception is caught and recorded rather than propagated).

**A genuine production bug, found while writing the `create_case` action test, not assumed from the coverage number alone:** `_action_create_case()` never set `Case.created_by`, which is `nullable=False` — the very first real test of this action hit a raw `IntegrityError` from SQLite. Worse than just failing that one action: `_execute_action()`'s `try/except Exception` around every action handler means this exception is normally swallowed and reported as `{"result": "error"}` rather than crashing the request — but by the time it's caught, `_action_create_case()`'s own `db.commit()` has already failed and left the session's underlying transaction aborted, which then poisons every subsequent operation in the same request (the `AutomationRuleExecution` row for this very action, and the route handler's own later commits) with a cascading `PendingRollbackError` — confirmed by reproducing the exact failure shape live via `git stash` before fixing it. Since `create_case` has been a selectable action type in the Rule Builder UI since Phase 4 with zero prior test coverage, any org that ever configured a rule with this action would have hit this on every matching event. Fixed with the one missing line (`created_by=actor_id`) — `actor_id` was already a function parameter, just never used.

**What did NOT change / what's still open:** `governance/training.py` (31%, largest raw gap), `dashboard.py` (21%), `risk_triggered_training_service.py` (11%), and the rest of the 15-30% band remain untouched — P54 is deliberately being worked one module at a time (per its own effort note: "not a blanket coverage-chasing pass"), not closed out in a single round. `decision_support_service.py` (0%, used only by `transactions.py`) and the two email vendor adapters (`email/resend.py`, `email/smtp.py`, both 0%) were noted during the usage-scan but not picked up this round — the email adapters need real vendor credentials to exercise meaningfully (same category as untested-without-live-keys code elsewhere in the codebase), and `decision_support_service.py`'s narrower single-caller footprint made it lower priority than the 4 originally-named modules.

**Verified:** all 49 new tests pass. `automation_engine.py` coverage 14%→96% (10 of 227 statements still missed, all minor edge branches — e.g. `create_calendar_item`'s explicit-due-date-vs-computed-default branch). Full suite: 734 passed, 2 skipped (685 + 49 new), 0 regressions. Coverage with CI's exact flags (`--cov-fail-under=58`): 73.68% (up from 72.88%). mypy: zero errors. Ruff (check + format, `app/`) clean.
**Detail:** `app/services/automation_engine.py`; `tests/test_automation_engine.py` (new).

## Stage 16 (Testing) — fifth pass, 2026-09-15 (P50b, "use nonce")

**Parked, then resolved on your direction:** P50 shipped `script-src 'self' 'unsafe-inline'` because the nonce alternative had a real, non-trivial cost (below) that wasn't yours to accept unilaterally. Asked you directly with the concrete tradeoff spelled out (what `unsafe-inline` actually risks, what a nonce buys, what it costs); you said "use nonce" — proceed and accept the site-wide dynamic-rendering cost.

**What changed:** `web/proxy.ts` (new) — a per-request nonce (`crypto.randomUUID()`, base64-encoded) is minted on every request and threaded two ways: into the `Content-Security-Policy` response header as `script-src 'self' 'nonce-<value>' 'strict-dynamic'` (replacing `'unsafe-inline'`), and into an `x-nonce` request header for the app to read. `app/layout.tsx` reads it via `next/headers`' `headers()` (now an `async` Server Component) and passes it to `next-themes`' `ThemeProvider`, the one inline `<script>` this app renders itself (its pre-paint theme-setting script, which accepts a `nonce` prop for exactly this). `'strict-dynamic'` lets Next.js's own nonce-tagged framework/page-bundle scripts load further scripts without needing every chunk URL individually allow-listed. `next.config.js`'s static `headers()` no longer sets CSP at all (a nonce can't be a build-time constant) — HSTS and the `/sw.js`/`/manifest.json` cache headers stay there, unchanged.

Built the file as `middleware.ts` first, matching P50's own earlier prototype — but Next 16 (already the pinned version) deprecated that convention mid-release in favour of `proxy.ts`/`export function proxy(...)` (same signature, same `config.matcher`, no other API change). Used the file conventions doc directly rather than the `unsafe-inline` shortcut of leaving a deprecation warning in CI output: renamed the file and its export, confirmed the deprecation warning is gone from a clean build.

**The cost, confirmed exactly as sized when this was first prototyped for P50:** every one of the ~60 previously static/SSG'd marketing and app routes is now `ƒ` (dynamic, server-rendered per request) in the build output — none are pre-rendered or CDN-cacheable anymore. This is the tradeoff you were asked about and accepted, not a surprise found after the fact.

**Verified, not just built and assumed correct:** `npx tsc --noEmit` clean. `npm run lint` — 0 errors (same pre-existing warnings as before, none newly introduced). `npm run build` — confirmed every route now shows `ƒ` (checked the full route list, not sampled). Started the real production server and checked a live response: `Content-Security-Policy` header carries a real random nonce (`curl -sI`), and the HTML body's actual `<script nonce="...">` tags — both Next's own framework/RSC-payload scripts and next-themes' inline theme script — carry that exact same value (`curl` + grep, not assumed from the header alone). Loaded the same 4 representative pages P50 checked (homepage, `/pricing`, `/dashboard`, `/solutions/vasp`) in real headless Chromium — zero CSP console violations on any of them, confirming `'strict-dynamic'` genuinely covers every script the app actually loads. Added a new e2e test (`e2e/marketing-pages.spec.ts`) asserting the shipped CSP is nonce-based (not `unsafe-inline`), that the header's nonce value matches a real inline `<script>` tag in that same response's body (not just present in the header), and that two independent page loads get two different nonces (proves it's minted fresh per request, not a cached/build-time value) — this becomes a permanent regression check, not a one-off manual verification. Full e2e suite (6 tests, up from 5): 5/5 clean consecutive runs, matching the flakiness-check discipline from P53's original CI fix. `npm run test` (unit/component): unaffected, 16/16 still pass.
**Detail:** `web/proxy.ts` (new, was briefly `middleware.ts` before the Next 16 rename); `web/app/layout.tsx`; `web/next.config.js`; `web/e2e/marketing-pages.spec.ts`.

## Stage 16 (Testing) — sixth pass, 2026-09-15 (P51, "fix now")

**Parked, then resolved on your direction:** P51 sat inside the broader KYC/ECDD hold by topic, not because it needed a vendor account/API key. Before touching it, asked you directly whether the offline review had happened or whether you specifically wanted this one item carved out — you said you wanted context first, so walked through the actual fields, why they weren't encrypted, what encrypting them for real would involve (existing-plaintext-row migration, key management), and confirmed no code currently searches/filters by these values (so non-deterministic encryption costs nothing functionally). You then said "fix now" / "lift the hold, implement P51 now" — an explicit, informed decision to carve this one item out, not a default.

**What changed:** `Customer.tax_identification_number`, `BeneficialOwner.tax_identification_number` and `BeneficialOwner.id_number` (`app/models/customer.py`) are now encrypted at rest.
- **Enforced at the ORM layer, not patched into call sites:** a new `EncryptedKycString` `TypeDecorator` (`app/services/crypto.py`) transparently encrypts on write / decrypts on read. This was a deliberate choice over hunting down each read/write site — found a `BeneficialOwner(**payload.model_dump(exclude_none=True))` dict-spread construction in `app/api/routes/customers.py` that would have silently written plaintext if encryption had been wired in at individual call sites instead of the column type itself.
- **A dedicated key, not reused from `STORAGE_ENCRYPTION_KEY`:** new `KYC_ENCRYPTION_KEY` setting (`app/config.py`), same dev-convenience fallback-to-`SECRET_KEY` pattern as the existing storage-credentials key, but rotatable independently — KYC identity numbers are a more sensitive class than connector credentials and shouldn't share a key with them. Uses its own `"kyc:"` prefix (vs. the existing `"enc:"`) so the two are never confusable.
- **Columns widened** `String(50)` → `String(255)` (a Fernet token is ~104+ chars, doesn't fit the old width).
- **The real one-time data migration**, not just a schema change: `alembic/versions/19838395f614_encrypt_kyc_identity_number_columns.py` re-encrypts every existing plaintext value in place, guarded by an idempotency check (skips anything already carrying the `"kyc:"` prefix) so it's safe to re-run.
- **A real bug caught by testing the migration against actual Postgres, not assumed correct from the code:** the first `downgrade()` tried to narrow the columns back to `VARCHAR(50)` — which is impossible once real encrypted data exists (a ~104-char token can't fit in 50 chars) and failed outright with `StringDataRightTruncation` when actually run. Since decrypting rows back to plaintext on a routine downgrade isn't something this migration should do silently to live KYC data either, fixed by making `downgrade()` a deliberate no-op (leaves both schema and data exactly as `upgrade()` left them) rather than a broken column-width revert.
- Updated `bulk_import.py`'s CSV column-mapping help text, which literally warned "do not store TFN in plain text in production systems" — now stale, since that's exactly what this closes.

**What did NOT change:** the broader KYC/ECDD/vendor-API-key hold from 2026-09-15 stays in effect for everything else (Sumsub, PEP screening, the Dow Jones adapter) — this was a narrow, explicitly-requested carve-out, not a reopening of that hold. No equality-filter/search functionality was lost — confirmed (grepped) no code anywhere filters on these fields' value, so Fernet's non-deterministic ciphertext (a real constraint: encrypting the same value twice produces different output, so these columns can never be used in a `WHERE column = value` query) costs nothing today.

**Verified, including a real bug found only by testing the migration against actual Postgres:** wrote real plaintext rows directly via SQL (simulating genuine pre-existing production data, not ORM-inserted-then-relabeled), ran the migration against a real local Postgres 16 instance, confirmed the raw row was re-encrypted (`kyc:` prefix, not the original value) and the ORM decrypts it back correctly in a fresh session. Confirmed idempotency by downgrading and re-upgrading — the already-encrypted value came back byte-for-byte unchanged, not double-encrypted. New `tests/test_p51_kyc_field_encryption_smoke.py` (8 tests): the crypto roundtrip functions directly (encrypt/decrypt, `None`/empty passthrough, legacy-plaintext passthrough for `decrypt_kyc_field`, two encryptions of the same value differing), plus request-level tests through the real API (`POST /customers`, `POST .../beneficial-owners`) that read the raw database row via SQL — bypassing the ORM's own decrypt-on-read — to prove the plaintext value a caller sent never actually reaches disk, not just that the ORM round-trips it correctly. Full suite: 742 passed, 2 skipped (734 + 8 new) against both real Postgres and default SQLite, 0 regressions. Coverage with CI's exact flags: 73.73% (gate 58%). `tests/test_migration_chain_smoke.py` re-run against Postgres to confirm the new migration chains cleanly (upgrade-from-empty + downgrade/re-upgrade) — both pass. mypy: zero errors. Ruff (check + format, `app/`) clean.
**Detail:** `app/models/customer.py`; `app/services/crypto.py`; `app/config.py`; `app/services/bulk_import.py`; `alembic/versions/19838395f614_encrypt_kyc_identity_number_columns.py` (new); `tests/test_p51_kyc_field_encryption_smoke.py` (new).
